- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 8/10

### CoLD: Counterfactually-Guided Length Debiasing for Process Reward Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15698v1)
- **Authors**: Congmin Zheng, Jiachen Zhu, Jianghao Lin, Xinyi Dai, Yong Yu, Weinan Zhang, Mengyue Yang
- **Abstract**: Process Reward Models (PRMs) play a central role in evaluating and guiding multi-step reasoning in large language models (LLMs), especially for mathematical problem solving. However, we identify a pervasive length bias in existing PRMs: they tend to assign higher scores to longer reasoning steps, even when the semantic content and logical validity are unchanged. This bias undermines the reliability of reward predictions and leads to overly verbose outputs during inference. To address this issue, we propose CoLD(Counterfactually-Guided Length Debiasing), a unified framework that mitigates length bias through three components: an explicit length-penalty adjustment, a learned bias estimator trained to capture spurious length-related signals, and a joint training strategy that enforces length-invariance in reward predictions. Our approach is grounded in counterfactual reasoning and informed by causal graph analysis. Extensive experiments on MATH500 and GSM-Plus show that CoLD consistently reduces reward-length correlation, improves accuracy in step selection, and encourages more concise, logically valid reasoning. These results demonstrate the effectiveness and practicality of CoLD in improving the fidelity and robustness of PRMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CoLD: Counterfactually-Guided Length Debiasing for Process Reward Models":

**Summary:**

The paper identifies and addresses a length bias in process reward models (PRMs), where PRMs tend to assign higher scores to longer reasoning steps, even if the semantic content and logical validity are the same.  The authors propose a Counterfactually-Guided Length Debiasing (CoLD) framework to mitigate this bias.  CoLD combines three components: an explicit length-penalty adjustment, a learned bias estimator, and a joint training strategy that encourages length-invariance in reward predictions. The approach is grounded in counterfactual reasoning and causal graph analysis. Experiments on MATH500 and GSM-Plus show that CoLD reduces reward-length correlation, improves step selection accuracy, and promotes more concise reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The identification of length bias as a significant problem in PRMs is a valuable contribution. While length bias is a known issue in other areas of NLP (e.g., RLHF), its focused analysis and mitigation within the specific context of *process reward models* represents a genuine advancement. Causal graph analysis to describe and model the confounding factors in PRMs adds to the methodological novelty. The combination of length penalty, bias estimator, and joint training, while not entirely individually novel, constitutes a novel *integrated* framework specifically tailored to the PRM context.

*   **Significance:** PRMs are becoming increasingly important for evaluating and guiding multi-step reasoning in large language models, especially for tasks like mathematical problem-solving. Improving the reliability and fairness of these models is therefore of considerable practical importance. The potential of CoLD to improve step-selection accuracy and encourage more concise and logical reasoning will improve the overall robustness of solutions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of length bias in PRMs and provides compelling empirical evidence for its existence.
    *   **Principled Approach:** The use of counterfactual reasoning and causal graph analysis provides a strong theoretical foundation for the proposed solution.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The combination of length penalty, bias estimator, and joint training addresses the problem from multiple angles, likely making the solution more robust.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The experimental results on MATH500 and GSM-Plus, and the component-wise ablation studies, support the effectiveness of CoLD in mitigating length bias and improving overall performance. The extension of the dataset to include semi-synthetic examples provides a means of controlled evaluation that reinforces the analysis.
    *   **Well-Written:** The paper is well-structured and clearly written, making it easy to understand the problem, the proposed solution, and the experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the paper shows improvements, the magnitude of improvement may be considered marginal.  Further, the baseline PRMs may not be representative of the very best PRMs, particularly those emerging from more recent work. However, the robust analysis of the reasons for, and ways of mitigating against, length bias, provides valuable insights in their own right.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework has multiple components (length penalty, bias estimator, joint training), and tuning hyperparameters for each component requires substantial computation. It could be hard to deploy in cases with limited training resources.
    *   **Generalizability:**  The experiments are limited to mathematical problem-solving. The generalizability of CoLD to other reasoning tasks or other types of PRMs should be considered.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence the development and application of future PRMs. The identification and analysis of length bias will encourage researchers to be more aware of this problem and to consider incorporating debiasing techniques into their own models.  The CoLD framework itself provides a solid foundation for further research in this area.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper addresses a real and important problem and provides a novel and well-validated solution, the improvements over existing methods are not dramatic. The complexity of the approach and the limited scope of evaluation also temper the significance.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Is Large Language Model Performance on Reasoning Tasks Impacted by Different Ways Questions Are Asked?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15707v1)
- **Authors**: Seok Hwan Song, Mohna Chakraborty, Qi Li, Wallapak Tavanapong
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have been evaluated using diverse question types, e.g., multiple-choice, true/false, and short/long answers. This study answers an unexplored question about the impact of different question types on LLM accuracy on reasoning tasks. We investigate the performance of five LLMs on three different types of questions using quantitative and deductive reasoning tasks. The performance metrics include accuracy in the reasoning steps and choosing the final answer. Key Findings: (1) Significant differences exist in LLM performance across different question types. (2) Reasoning accuracy does not necessarily correlate with the final selection accuracy. (3) The number of options and the choice of words, influence LLM performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of different question types (short answer, multiple-choice, and true/false) on the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on quantitative and deductive reasoning tasks. The authors evaluate five LLMs using quantitative reasoning problems from the GSM8K dataset and deductive reasoning problems from a modified bAbI dataset. They analyze the LLMs' final selection accuracy and reasoning accuracy. The key findings include significant performance variations across question types, a disconnect between reasoning and selection accuracy, and the influence of option number and word choices on performance in multiple-choice and true/false formats. The paper identifies patterns of incorrect outputs and explores factors influencing LLM performance on different question types.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive examination of how question types impact LLM performance specifically focusing on both reasoning and final selection accuracy. While previous works have touched on factors affecting MCQ performance, this study systematically compares SAQs, MCQs, and TFQs and their influence on both aspects. The specific research questions and experimental design are largely unexplored, especially the combined investigation of reasoning steps and final answers. The inclusion of the "Something else" option in MCQs is a minor contribution adding solution space for reasoning in MCQs.

*   **Significance:** Understanding how LLMs perform under different question types is crucial for designing better benchmarks and interpreting LLM evaluation results. The finding that reasoning accuracy doesn't always correlate with selection accuracy is significant, pointing out that LLMs can arrive at the correct answer through faulty reasoning (or vice versa) and thus a better understanding of the biases within the models is necessary. The detailed error analysis, focusing on reasons behind incorrect selections, provide valuable insights to improve LLM architectures and prompt engineering. The findings on MCQ factors (like number of options and "Something else") and TFQ factors (instruction type and "True" vs. "False" bias) can guide future benchmark design to be more fair and accurate. The investigation on open- and closed-source LLMs can bring the attention of how different families of LLMs perform on question types. The detailed analysis presented in the study has implications for designing question types and designing more robust methods to assess LLM performance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Systematic comparison of three question types.
    *   Analysis of both reasoning and final selection accuracy.
    *   Identification of factors influencing MCQ and TFQ performance.
    *   Detailed error analysis and categorization.
    *   Use of multiple LLMs (both closed and open source), increasing generalizability.
    *   Focus on quantitative and deductive reasoning tasks, crucial for many real-world applications.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The study relies on only two datasets, limiting the generalizability of findings. While these datasets are established reasoning benchmarks, expanding to others (e.g., more diverse reasoning tasks, different domains) would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   Manual evaluation is time-consuming, meaning a smaller number of problems could be rigorously assessed.
    *   While the paper mentions that the models are run in a zero-shot setting, there could have been an examination of prompting methods and their effect on question type performance.
    *   Some of the analysis, while thorough, is descriptive, listing frequencies without offering clear solutions to mitigate the identified biases or errors.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings can influence how LLM benchmarks are constructed and used. It highlights the importance of considering question type as a factor and calls for more nuanced evaluation metrics. The error analysis can inspire research into improving LLM reasoning and selection accuracy, especially in situations where these metrics diverge. The insights into MCQ and TFQ design can lead to more fair and robust assessments.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper provides a systematic investigation of an important topic. It’s well-executed, provides valuable empirical evidence, and generates insights that are directly applicable to LLM benchmark design. However, the limitations in dataset scope and the lack of concrete solutions to the revealed challenges slightly weaken the overall impact.

### DiffPF: Differentiable Particle Filtering with Generative Sampling via Conditional Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15716v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyu Wan, Lin Zhao
- **Abstract**: This paper proposes DiffPF, a differentiable particle filter that leverages diffusion models for state estimation in dynamic systems. Unlike conventional differentiable particle filters, which require importance weighting and typically rely on predefined or low-capacity proposal distributions. DiffPF learns a flexible posterior sampler by conditioning a diffusion model on predicted particles and the current observation. This enables accurate, equally-weighted sampling from complex, high-dimensional, and multimodal filtering distributions. We evaluate DiffPF across a range of scenarios, including both unimodal and highly multimodal distributions, and test it on simulated as well as real-world tasks, where it consistently outperforms existing filtering baselines. In particular, DiffPF achieves an 82.8% improvement in estimation accuracy on a highly multimodal global localization benchmark, and a 26% improvement on the real-world KITTI visual odometry benchmark, compared to state-of-the-art differentiable filters. To the best of our knowledge, DiffPF is the first method to integrate conditional diffusion models into particle filtering, enabling high-quality posterior sampling that produces more informative particles and significantly improves state estimation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffPF, a novel differentiable particle filter that integrates conditional diffusion models for state estimation in dynamic systems. Unlike traditional differentiable particle filters, DiffPF replaces the hand-designed proposal distributions and importance weighting with a learned denoising process (using a conditional diffusion model) for equally-weighted sampling. This allows for sampling from more complex, high-dimensional, and multimodal filtering distributions. The method is evaluated on synthetic and real-world tasks, demonstrating improved state estimation accuracy compared to existing differentiable filtering baselines, particularly in scenarios involving multimodal distributions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of conditional diffusion models into a differentiable particle filter framework. While diffusion models have seen application in other areas of robotics (e.g., policy learning), this paper presents a novel application in the context of state estimation and differentiable particle filtering. This is significant because it addresses a key limitation of existing DPFs: the difficulty in designing effective proposal distributions and the reliance on importance sampling, which often fail in high-dimensional or multimodal scenarios. The approach of equally weighted particles through iterative denoising is a clever way to avoid resampling and weight degeneracy issues. Using U-Net based diffusion model architecture is also well-established. However, the architecture itself may not be the core novelty.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve state estimation accuracy and robustness in complex, real-world systems. The experimental results showing improvements over strong baselines (NF-DPFs in particular) on visual odometry and global localization tasks support this claim. The results show the effectiveness of the proposed method in generating high quality particles, that in turn improve state estimation accuracy. The ablation studies are well designed and shed light on the contribution of individual components. The results on the KITTI dataset are especially compelling, indicating practical relevance. The elimination of resampling and dependence on handcrafted proposals are considerable advantages.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The integration of diffusion models into DPFs addresses a key limitation of existing methods.
    *   The equally-weighted particle approach avoids resampling and weight degeneracy.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in state estimation accuracy, particularly in multimodal scenarios.
    *   Comprehensive experiments over various simulated and real-world scenarios.
    *   Well-written and clearly explains the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The computational cost of diffusion models can be a concern, even with the relatively small number of diffusion steps used. While the paper reports inference frequencies, a more detailed analysis of computational bottlenecks would be beneficial. The implementation complexity of diffusion models might limit adoption compared to simpler filters.
    *   The paper mainly compares against existing differentiable filters. It would be insightful to compare against non-differentiable particle filters, particularly in multimodal scenarios where these methods can perform well with careful proposal design.
    *   Although results are better, experiments on the KITTI dataset were restricted only to the sequences of the KITTI dataset and do not provide details regarding generalization capabilities, or challenges due to loop closure.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of state estimation by introducing a new and effective way to design differentiable particle filters. The use of diffusion models opens up new avenues for learning complex posterior distributions and improving robustness in challenging scenarios. Other researchers may apply similar integration strategy to other state estimation methodologies. Further improvements could lead to more robust state estimation in diverse robotics applications.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of differentiable particle filtering by integrating conditional diffusion models. The strengths of the approach in addressing limitations of existing DPFs, along with strong empirical results, justify a high score. The weaknesses regarding computational cost, limited comparative analysis against non-differentiable filters, and the lack of generalization analysis are important considerations but do not outweigh the overall contribution. The paper has the potential to influence future research in state estimation and robotics.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 8/10

### BEnchmarking LLMs for Ophthalmology (BELO) for Ophthalmological Knowledge and Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15717v1)
- **Authors**: Sahana Srinivasan, Xuguang Ai, Thaddaeus Wai Soon Lo, Aidan Gilson, Minjie Zou, Ke Zou, Hyunjae Kim, Mingjia Yang, Krithi Pushpanathan, Samantha Yew, Wan Ting Loke, Jocelyn Goh, Yibing Chen, Yiming Kong, Emily Yuelei Fu, Michelle Ongyong Hui, Kristen Nwanyanwu, Amisha Dave, Kelvin Zhenghao Li, Chen-Hsin Sun, Mark Chia, Gabriel Dawei Yang, Wendy Meihua Wong, David Ziyou Chen, Dianbo Liu, Maxwell Singer, Fares Antaki, Lucian V Del Priore, Jost Jonas, Ron Adelman, Qingyu Chen, Yih-Chung Tham
- **Abstract**: Current benchmarks evaluating large language models (LLMs) in ophthalmology are limited in scope and disproportionately prioritise accuracy. We introduce BELO (BEnchmarking LLMs for Ophthalmology), a standardized and comprehensive evaluation benchmark developed through multiple rounds of expert checking by 13 ophthalmologists. BELO assesses ophthalmology-related clinical accuracy and reasoning quality. Using keyword matching and a fine-tuned PubMedBERT model, we curated ophthalmology-specific multiple-choice-questions (MCQs) from diverse medical datasets (BCSC, MedMCQA, MedQA, BioASQ, and PubMedQA). The dataset underwent multiple rounds of expert checking. Duplicate and substandard questions were systematically removed. Ten ophthalmologists refined the explanations of each MCQ's correct answer. This was further adjudicated by three senior ophthalmologists. To illustrate BELO's utility, we evaluated six LLMs (OpenAI o1, o3-mini, GPT-4o, DeepSeek-R1, Llama-3-8B, and Gemini 1.5 Pro) using accuracy, macro-F1, and five text-generation metrics (ROUGE-L, BERTScore, BARTScore, METEOR, and AlignScore). In a further evaluation involving human experts, two ophthalmologists qualitatively reviewed 50 randomly selected outputs for accuracy, comprehensiveness, and completeness. BELO consists of 900 high-quality, expert-reviewed questions aggregated from five sources: BCSC (260), BioASQ (10), MedMCQA (572), MedQA (40), and PubMedQA (18). A public leaderboard has been established to promote transparent evaluation and reporting. Importantly, the BELO dataset will remain a hold-out, evaluation-only benchmark to ensure fair and reproducible comparisons of future models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the BELO paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BELO (BEnchmarking LLMs for Ophthalmology), a new benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the performance of large language models (LLMs) in ophthalmology.  BELO consists of 900 expert-reviewed, multiple-choice questions sourced from diverse medical datasets and rigorously vetted by ophthalmologists.  The authors demonstrate BELO's utility by evaluating six LLMs (OpenAI models, DeepSeek-R1, Llama-3-8B, and Gemini 1.5 Pro) using accuracy, macro-F1, and various text-generation metrics.  They also perform a qualitative evaluation with ophthalmologists reviewing the reasoning provided by selected models.  The results indicate that current LLMs, while achieving high accuracy, still have room for improvement in clinical reasoning within ophthalmology.  A public leaderboard is established for transparent comparison of future models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of a specialized benchmark like BELO for ophthalmology is a valuable step.  While existing medical LLM benchmarks exist, they often lack the granularity or expert validation needed for specific subspecialties. The paper's strength lies in the meticulous process of dataset curation and validation by a panel of ophthalmologists, ensuring clinical relevance and accuracy. The comparison to previously existing benchmarks highlight the need for domain specificity, however, the authors fail to rigorously show, with specific examples, why questions of other broader medical benchmarks are ill-suited for testing opthalmology.

*   **Significance:** BELO has the potential to significantly impact the field by providing a standardized and reliable tool for assessing LLMs in ophthalmology. This could accelerate the development of AI-powered tools for clinical decision support, education, and research in ophthalmology. By establishing a public leaderboard, the paper also promotes open and transparent model development.
* **Limitations and Weaknesses:**
*   **Limited question format:** The paper's focus on multiple-choice questions, while facilitating quantitative evaluation, restricts the assessment of more complex reasoning and problem-solving abilities. More diverse question formats, such as open-ended questions requiring paragraph-length answers, would improve its evaluation quality.
*   **Limited exploration of vision based LLMs:** Although the paper mentions it, it doesn't include testing of the visual reasoning for ophthalmology. This makes it difficult to access the benchmark's comprehensiveness.
*   **Lack of comparison with existing approaches:** The paper lacks a detailed comparison with similar projects, like Eyecare-Bench and Multi-OphthaLingua, in order to provide justification as to why the added effort for the BELO benchmark results in a significantly improved contribution.
*   **Model selection:** The study primarily evaluated a specific set of LLMs. A more comprehensive evaluation with a wider range of architectures and training methodologies would provide a more complete picture of the landscape.
*   **Generalizability:** Although the dataset curation has ophthalmological experts, the reliance on medical exam questions and pre-trained language models, as a way to obtain the data, reduces the contribution's ability to assess the true clinical reasoning required for more specialized ophthalmological tasks.

*   **Impact:**  BELO can serve as a valuable resource for researchers and developers in AI and ophthalmology. The dataset and leaderboard are likely to stimulate further research in this area.

*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology and results. The figures and tables are informative and contribute to the overall understanding.

**Justification of Score:**

While BELO represents a valuable contribution by providing a specialized and rigorously curated benchmark for LLMs in ophthalmology, its limitations prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The focus on MCQ question formats, the lack of visual inputs, the limited model selection, and the restricted task types (focused on pre-existing medical knowledge) limit its applicability to real-world clinical scenarios, reducing the impact to the field. A stronger emphasis on the advantages of the method, by providing justification and examples to the lack of quality of previously existing methods, would enhance the novelty as well. However, the rigorous curation and the establishment of a public leaderboard will foster future research.

### A Practical Investigation of Spatially-Controlled Image Generation with Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15724v1)
- **Authors**: Guoxuan Xia, Harleen Hanspal, Petru-Daniel Tudosiu, Shifeng Zhang, Sarah Parisot
- **Abstract**: Enabling image generation models to be spatially controlled is an important area of research, empowering users to better generate images according to their own fine-grained specifications via e.g. edge maps, poses. Although this task has seen impressive improvements in recent times, a focus on rapidly producing stronger models has come at the cost of detailed and fair scientific comparison. Differing training data, model architectures and generation paradigms make it difficult to disentangle the factors contributing to performance. Meanwhile, the motivations and nuances of certain approaches become lost in the literature. In this work, we aim to provide clear takeaways across generation paradigms for practitioners wishing to develop transformer-based systems for spatially-controlled generation, clarifying the literature and addressing knowledge gaps. We perform controlled experiments on ImageNet across diffusion-based/flow-based and autoregressive (AR) models. First, we establish control token prefilling as a simple, general and performant baseline approach for transformers. We then investigate previously underexplored sampling time enhancements, showing that extending classifier-free guidance to control, as well as softmax truncation, have a strong impact on control-generation consistency. Finally, we re-clarify the motivation of adapter-based approaches, demonstrating that they mitigate "forgetting" and maintain generation quality when trained on limited downstream data, but underperform full training in terms of generation-control consistency. Code will be released upon publication.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a practical investigation into spatially-controlled image generation using transformers.  It focuses on providing clear takeaways for practitioners, addressing gaps in the existing literature regarding fair comparisons, sampling enhancements, and the role of adapters versus full training. Through controlled experiments on ImageNet using both diffusion/flow-based (SiT) and autoregressive (VAR) models, the authors establish control token prefilling as a strong baseline. They then investigate the impact of different sampling techniques like classifier-free guidance (CFG) extended to control (ctrl-G) and softmax truncation. They also analyze and clarify the motivation behind using adapters for spatial control, particularly in mitigating "forgetting" when training on limited data.  The key findings highlight the effectiveness of prefilling, the benefits and trade-offs of different sampling strategies, and the specific advantages of adapters in low-data scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper doesn't introduce entirely new architectures or algorithms but focuses on a systematic comparison and practical evaluation of existing methods. The novelty lies primarily in:

    *   A rigorous comparative analysis of different spatial control techniques for transformer-based image generation.
    *   A detailed investigation into the effects of sampling parameters like CFG and softmax truncation on both generation quality and control consistency, which has been largely under-explored in prior work.
    *   A clear re-evaluation and clarification of the motivation for using adapters, highlighting their benefit in mitigating forgetting when training on limited data.
    *   The establishment of control token prefilling as a strong general baseline.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its practical value and contribution to the understanding of spatially-controlled image generation. It directly addresses issues that hinder practitioners from developing their systems effectively:

    *   **Clarity and Fair Comparisons:** The paper tackles the problem of inconsistent comparisons across different papers by conducting controlled experiments on a unified dataset, model architectures, and generation paradigms. This helps disentangle the factors contributing to performance and allows for more informed design choices.
    *   **Sampling Enhancements:** By exploring and comparing different sampling parameters, the paper provides valuable insights into how these parameters can be adjusted to improve generation quality and control consistency at inference time, without requiring retraining.
    *   **Adapter vs Full Training:** The paper clarifies the motivation for using adapters, which is particularly relevant in scenarios where the pre-trained model should be preserved or the training data is limited.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Controlled Experiments:** The use of controlled experiments allows for a rigorous and systematic evaluation of different techniques.
    *   **Clear Takeaways:** The paper is well-written and provides clear and actionable takeaways for practitioners.
    *   **Practical Value:** The focus on practical implementation and evaluation makes the paper highly relevant for developers and researchers working in the field.
    *   **Addressing Knowledge Gaps:** The paper effectively addresses gaps in the existing literature, providing a more comprehensive understanding of spatially-controlled image generation.
    * Reproducibility is enhanced by releasing code.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses primarily on ImageNet, which may not fully capture the complexities of real-world image generation tasks.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** While the paper provides valuable insights, the improvements achieved through sampling parameter tuning are incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   **Lack of New Techniques:** The paper doesn't introduce entirely new architectures or algorithms but focuses on evaluating and comparing existing methods. This could be seen as a limitation in terms of theoretical novelty.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of spatially-controlled image generation systems by providing practitioners with clear guidelines and practical recommendations. It can help researchers make more informed design choices and focus their efforts on areas that have the most significant impact. The re-evaluation of adapters and the identification of prefilling as a good baseline are valuable contributions.

*Score: 7.5*

**Rationale:** The paper provides a solid contribution by improving our understanding and facilitating practical implementation of spatially-controlled image generation. While it lacks radical novelty in terms of new architectures, the paper's focus on controlled comparisons, exploration of sampling parameters, and clarification of adapter motivations is very valuable and directly addresses the needs of the practitioners in the field. The insights are well-supported by experimental results, and the clear takeaways make the paper a useful resource for anyone working on this topic. The paper is a high-quality engineering effort that makes current research results more accessible. The scope could be a bit broader, and the improvements, while significant, are not paradigm-shifting.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### TokensGen: Harnessing Condensed Tokens for Long Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15728v1)
- **Authors**: Wenqi Ouyang, Zeqi Xiao, Danni Yang, Yifan Zhou, Shuai Yang, Lei Yang, Jianlou Si, Xingang Pan
- **Abstract**: Generating consistent long videos is a complex challenge: while diffusion-based generative models generate visually impressive short clips, extending them to longer durations often leads to memory bottlenecks and long-term inconsistency. In this paper, we propose TokensGen, a novel two-stage framework that leverages condensed tokens to address these issues. Our method decomposes long video generation into three core tasks: (1) inner-clip semantic control, (2) long-term consistency control, and (3) inter-clip smooth transition. First, we train To2V (Token-to-Video), a short video diffusion model guided by text and video tokens, with a Video Tokenizer that condenses short clips into semantically rich tokens. Second, we introduce T2To (Text-to-Token), a video token diffusion transformer that generates all tokens at once, ensuring global consistency across clips. Finally, during inference, an adaptive FIFO-Diffusion strategy seamlessly connects adjacent clips, reducing boundary artifacts and enhancing smooth transitions. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach significantly enhances long-term temporal and content coherence without incurring prohibitive computational overhead. By leveraging condensed tokens and pre-trained short video models, our method provides a scalable, modular solution for long video generation, opening new possibilities for storytelling, cinematic production, and immersive simulations. Please see our project page at https://vicky0522.github.io/tokensgen-webpage/ .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TokensGen, a novel two-stage framework for generating long, consistent videos from text prompts.  It addresses the challenges of both long-term consistency and high computational demands associated with extending short video diffusion models to longer durations. TokensGen achieves this by:

1.  **Inner-clip Semantic Control (To2V Model):**  A short video diffusion model (based on CogVideoX) is trained to generate semantically rich clips guided by both text and "video tokens."  A Video Tokenizer encodes short clips into these condensed tokens, providing richer semantic information than text alone.
2.  **Long-term Consistency Control (T2To Model):** A video token diffusion transformer (T2To) is trained to generate the *entire* sequence of video tokens for a minute-long video, ensuring global consistency across all clips. This operates in a more compact token space.
3.  **Inter-clip Smooth Transitions (Adaptive FIFO-Diffusion):** An adaptive FIFO-Diffusion strategy is used during inference to connect adjacent clips seamlessly, reducing boundary artifacts and enhancing smooth transitions. The adaptive component addresses distributional shifts caused by naive padding, resulting in improved frame quality.

The authors demonstrate that TokensGen significantly enhances long-term temporal and content coherence without incurring prohibitive computational overhead, providing a scalable and modular solution for long video generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel combination of techniques. The core idea of using condensed video tokens to represent longer videos is not entirely new, but the specific two-stage architecture with To2V and T2To models, along with the adaptive FIFO diffusion strategy, demonstrates innovation. However, the techniques are building on top of existing diffusion models and frameworks.  The video tokenizer and Transformer architecture itself are established concepts. The originality lies in the *application* and *combination* of these concepts for the specific task of long video generation.

*   **Significance:**  Long video generation is an important and challenging problem in the field.  TokensGen offers a potentially significant step forward by addressing the long-term consistency and memory bottlenecks that plague existing methods. The modularity of the approach is a key strength, allowing for seamless integration with other existing short-term control strategies. The claim of scalability is significant, as it suggests that this method can potentially be applied to even longer videos or more complex scenes in the future. The use of pre-trained models makes the approach more practical, potentially lowering the barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners in the field. The ability to directly edit existing videos is a welcome advantage and opens up various applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges in long video generation and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Well-Defined Architecture:** The TokenGen architecture is well-defined and the roles of each component are clearly explained.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper demonstrates strong quantitative and qualitative results, comparing TokensGen to a range of baseline methods. The human evaluation results are particularly compelling.
    *   **Modularity:** The modular design allows for flexibility and integration with other techniques.
    *   **Scalability:** The use of condensed tokens is designed to improve the method’s overall scalability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Datasets:** The model is primarily tested on game-play and landscape video datasets, meaning its performance on datasets showing faces and bodies may be limited.
    *   **Token fidelity:** Due to the compression in the Video Tokens, fine-grained detail is not preserved as well as when the uncompressed original footage is used. This is highlighted in the Limitations and Discussions section and is demonstrated by some of the results shown in the appendix.
    *   **Computational cost:** While the paper claims computational efficiency, the absolute computational cost compared to other long video generation methods is unclear. While the number of floating point operations for this method are not as significant, other aspects of computational cost are not fully addressed.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the claims of scalability and modularity hold up in future work, TokensGen could significantly influence the field of long video generation. It offers a promising approach for balancing long-term consistency, computational efficiency, and high-quality results.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the method introduces a novel technique, it relies heavily on existing short video generation models, existing Video Tokenization techniques, and a Transformer architecture. The true innovation is in how these are combined, and the introduction of the adaptive FIFO Diffusion strategy. The results are positive and show the effectiveness of the method, but there are notable limitations. Therefore, a high score is not warranted.

### Gaze-supported Large Language Model Framework for Bi-directional Human-Robot Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15729v1)
- **Authors**: Jens V. Rüppel, Andrey Rudenko, Tim Schreiter, Martin Magnusson, Achim J. Lilienthal
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) creates an exciting potential for flexible, general knowledge-driven Human-Robot Interaction (HRI) systems for assistive robots. Existing HRI systems demonstrate great progress in interpreting and following user instructions, action generation, and robot task solving. On the other hand, bi-directional, multi-modal, and context-aware support of the user in collaborative tasks still remains an open challenge. In this paper, we present a gaze- and speech-informed interface to the assistive robot, which is able to perceive the working environment from multiple vision inputs and support the dynamic user in their tasks. Our system is designed to be modular and transferable to adapt to diverse tasks and robots, and it is capable of real-time use of language-based interaction state representation and fast on board perception modules. Its development was supported by multiple public dissemination events, contributing important considerations for improved robustness and user experience. Furthermore, in two lab studies, we compare the performance and user ratings of our system with those of a traditional scripted HRI pipeline. Our findings indicate that an LLM-based approach enhances adaptability and marginally improves user engagement and task execution metrics but may produce redundant output, while a scripted pipeline is well suited for more straightforward tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a novel framework for bi-directional human-robot interaction (HRI) that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs), gaze tracking, and speech recognition. The system aims to provide assistive robots with a more flexible and context-aware way to collaborate with human users in dynamic environments. The framework uses an LLM-based reasoning module to generate robot action plans from fused multimodal inputs (vision, gaze, speech) and can be adapted to different robot platforms. The authors validate their framework through both public dissemination events and a controlled lab study, comparing its performance against a traditional scripted HRI pipeline in a manufacturing context.  They found that the LLM-based approach enhances adaptability and potentially improves user engagement, although it might introduce redundancy and increased computational costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper demonstrates reasonable novelty in combining several existing technologies in a novel way. The use of an LLM for HRI is not entirely new, but the integration of gaze tracking and a multi-modal input fusion approach in a dynamic manufacturing environment represents an advance. The focus on bi-directional communication, where the robot provides feedback and intent, also strengthens the novelty. The implementation of the framework with modularity in mind, allowing it to be applied to different robots by only modifying the robot SDK function calls, is a strong contribution towards transferability.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research exploring the use of LLMs in HRI. While the results of the controlled experiments show only marginal improvements in user engagement and task execution compared to the scripted pipeline, the framework's adaptability and potential for handling complex scenarios hold promise. The detailed architecture description and the insights gained from public dissemination events are valuable for other researchers in the field.
However, the actual gains over scripted approaches are not substantial and the cost increases are substantial. The paper points out some of the challenges using LLMs, but does not fully quantify trade-offs such as computational burden or energy-costs.
**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Architecture:** The paper provides a clear and well-structured explanation of the framework architecture and its individual modules.
*   **Multimodal Input Fusion:** The integration of gaze tracking, speech recognition, and computer vision enhances the robot's awareness of the user's state and the environment.
*   **Modularity:** The modular design promotes transferability and adaptability to different robot platforms and tasks.
*   **Validation:** The paper includes both public dissemination events and a controlled lab study to validate the framework.
*   **Real-world applicability:** Addressing challenges in complex industrial or healthcare environments highlights the system's potential practical impact.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Marginal Performance Improvements:** The lab study shows only marginal improvements over the scripted pipeline in some metrics, and none in others. The claimed increase in adaptability of the LLM is not clearly quantified.
*   **Limited Generalization:** The evaluation focuses on a specific task (pick-and-place) in a structured environment. The results may not generalize to more complex or unstructured tasks.
*   **Computational Cost:** The LLM-based approach consumes more energy, which is a significant factor for mobile robots. This is mentioned, but not discussed in detail. The exact cost of the interaction is not detailed (money, time, compute cycles).
*   **Redundancy in output**: The authors admit to redundancy, but downplay the overall effect.
*   **Missing comparative results**: All study participants had prior experience with scripted systems, meaning that they may not be a good cohort for measuring improvements.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper can influence future research in HRI by demonstrating the feasibility of using LLMs for bi-directional communication and by providing insights into the challenges and trade-offs involved. The modular design and detailed architecture description can serve as a starting point for other researchers developing similar systems. However, the limited performance gains and increased costs may temper enthusiasm for LLM-based approaches, especially for simple tasks.
The paper also points out some of the challenges using LLMs, such as computational burden, hallucination, and energy-costs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is reasonably novel in its implementation, and the experimental evaluations reveal some improvements over scripted systems. However, the performance gains are limited and have significant energy costs.

### Understanding Large Language Models' Ability on Interdisciplinary Research
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15736v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanhao Shen, Daniel Xavier de Sousa, Ricardo Marçal, Ali Asad, Hongyu Guo, Xiaodan Zhu
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have revealed their impressive ability to perform multi-step, logic-driven reasoning across complex domains, positioning them as powerful tools and collaborators in scientific discovery while challenging the long-held view that inspiration-driven ideation is uniquely human. However, the lack of a dedicated benchmark that evaluates LLMs' ability to develop ideas in Interdisciplinary Research (IDR) settings poses a critical barrier to fully understanding their strengths and limitations. To address this gap, we introduce IDRBench -- a pioneering benchmark featuring an expert annotated dataset and a suite of tasks tailored to evaluate LLMs' capabilities in proposing valuable research ideas from different scientific domains for interdisciplinary research. This benchmark aims to provide a systematic framework for assessing LLM performance in complex, cross-domain scientific research. Our dataset consists of scientific publications sourced from the ArXiv platform covering six distinct disciplines, and is annotated by domain experts with diverse academic backgrounds. To ensure high-quality annotations, we emphasize clearly defined dimensions that characterize authentic interdisciplinary research. The design of evaluation tasks in IDRBench follows a progressive, real-world perspective, reflecting the natural stages of interdisciplinary research development, including 1) IDR Paper Identification, 2) IDR Idea Integration, and 3) IDR Idea Recommendation. Using IDRBench, we construct baselines across 10 LLMs and observe that despite fostering some level of IDR awareness, LLMs still struggle to produce quality IDR ideas. These findings could not only spark new research directions, but also help to develop next-generation LLMs that excel in interdisciplinary research.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and novelty/significance score for the paper, maintaining a rigorous and critical stance.

**Summary:**

The paper "Understanding Large Language Models' Ability on Interdisciplinary Research" introduces IDRBench, a new benchmark designed to assess Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of interdisciplinary research (IDR). The authors argue that existing benchmarks lack specific evaluation of LLMs' capacity for IDR, particularly the integration of knowledge across distinct disciplines. IDRBench includes a human-annotated dataset of scientific publications from arXiv, structured as knowledge triplets representing interdisciplinary connections. The benchmark includes three tasks: (1) IDR Paper Identification (IPI), (2) IDR Idea Integration (I3), and (3) IDR Idea Recommendation (I2R). The authors evaluated ten mainstream LLMs using IDRBench and found that while LLMs demonstrate some IDR awareness, they struggle to produce high-quality IDR ideas. The paper concludes that these findings can inspire future research directions toward developing LLMs that excel in interdisciplinary contexts. The dataset and code are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a gap:** The paper identifies a clear and important gap in LLM evaluation – the assessment of interdisciplinary reasoning. The motivation for a specialized benchmark is well-articulated.
*   **Well-defined Benchmark:** IDRBench provides a well-structured and carefully considered benchmark to evaluate LLMs for IDR. The three tasks build upon each other logically to represent the progressive stages in interdisciplinary research.
*   **Human-Annotated Dataset:** The use of a human-annotated dataset is crucial for assessing the nuanced and often subjective nature of interdisciplinary research. The authors explain their annotation process clearly, and discuss the choice to sample both positive and negative instances.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors evaluated a variety of mainstream LLMs on their benchmark, thus establishing baseline results. The analysis of different models helps to understand the various strengths and weaknesses of those LLMs for IDR tasks.
*   **Public Availability:** Releasing the dataset and code promotes reproducibility and encourages further research in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Scale:** While the human annotation is valuable, the size of the positive set of the dataset (120 instances) is relatively small. This may limit the generalizability of the findings and the ability to train/fine-tune models effectively using IDRBench itself. The negative dataset contains more examples (over 9,000), but is created using stratified random sampling and may lack more challenging "near-miss" examples.
*   **Dataset Source:** Limiting the dataset to arXiv papers is a reasonable decision in many respects. However, it may introduce a bias towards certain scientific fields and styles of research.
*   **Task Simplifications:** Although I acknowledge that the three IDR tasks were designed based on the real world scenario of human researchers conducting IDR, the three proposed tasks are somewhat simplified. Real-world IDR often involves a more iterative and explorative process. A single triplet may not fully capture the complexities that occur in a much broader context.
*   **Limited Qualitative Analysis:** While the authors present some qualitative analysis (Appendix A.3), expanding this with more examples of LLM's reasoning and creative problem-solving failures would further enrich the paper's insights.
*   **Lack of Comparison with Existing LLM Benchmarks:** While the authors discussed how IDRBench is unique compared to existing benchmarks, this work would benefit from more clearly relating the LLM performance on IDRBench to results on established reasoning benchmarks like BIG-Bench, MMLU, or ARC. This would provide context on how much the LLMs struggle in general reasoning tasks or specific challenges for IDR tasks.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in:

*   **The concept and design of IDRBench as a dedicated benchmark for interdisciplinary research.**  This is a novel contribution that fills a notable gap in the LLM evaluation landscape.
*   **The systematic evaluation of various LLMs for IDR capabilities.** While IDR is an important aspect of future LLM research, this is the first work that tackles this gap.
*   **The structured, human-annotated IDR dataset.** The combination of multiple disciplines provides the basis for testing interdisciplinary capability in a way that other current benchmarks do not.

The paper is significant because:

*   It highlights the limitations of current LLMs in addressing interdisciplinary problems, guiding future development efforts.
*   It provides a tool and a framework for systematically evaluating progress in this important area.
*   It can potentially facilitate the development of LLMs that are better equipped to contribute to scientific discovery and innovation in interdisciplinary fields.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying a gap in LLM evaluation and proposing a novel benchmark to address it. The design of IDRBench, the use of human annotation, and the comprehensive evaluation of multiple LLMs are all significant strengths. However, the limitations of the dataset scale, the simplifications in tasks, and the limited qualitative analysis slightly temper the overall score. The paper presents a solid foundation, setting the stage for future research and improvement of IDRBench and LLMs in interdisciplinary contexts. The novelty is strong, and the potential impact on the field of LLM development is considerable.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Appearance Harmonization via Bilateral Grid Prediction with Transformers for 3DGS
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15748v1)
- **Authors**: Jisu Shin, Richard Shaw, Seunghyun Shin, Anton Pelykh, Zhensong Zhang, Hae-Gon Jeon, Eduardo Perez-Pellitero
- **Abstract**: Modern camera pipelines apply extensive on-device processing, such as exposure adjustment, white balance, and color correction, which, while beneficial individually, often introduce photometric inconsistencies across views. These appearance variations violate multi-view consistency and degrade the quality of novel view synthesis. Joint optimization of scene representations and per-image appearance embeddings has been proposed to address this issue, but at the cost of increased computational complexity and slower training. In this work, we propose a transformer-based method that predicts spatially adaptive bilateral grids to correct photometric variations in a multi-view consistent manner, enabling robust cross-scene generalization without the need for scene-specific retraining. By incorporating the learned grids into the 3D Gaussian Splatting pipeline, we improve reconstruction quality while maintaining high training efficiency. Extensive experiments show that our approach outperforms or matches existing scene-specific optimization methods in reconstruction fidelity and convergence speed.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Appearance Harmonization via Bilateral Grid Prediction with Transformers for 3DGS":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of photometric inconsistencies in multi-view images used for 3D reconstruction with 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS). These inconsistencies, stemming from in-camera processing or lighting variations, degrade the quality of novel view synthesis. The authors propose a transformer-based approach to predict spatially adaptive bilateral grids for each input view. These grids are then used to correct the photometric variations in a multi-view consistent manner, aligning all views to a reference view's appearance. The key advantages of their method are that it's generalizable (no scene-specific optimization), efficient (constant computational cost per frame), and decoupled from the 3DGS optimization process itself. They also introduce per-image bilateral confidence grids to handle challenging regions like over/under-exposure. Experiments show improvements in reconstruction quality and convergence speed compared to existing methods that jointly optimize appearance embeddings with scene geometry.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. The core idea of using transformers for multi-view appearance harmonization is a solid contribution, however, the application of bilateral grids itself is not a novel construct as they have been previously utilized in work like BilaRF. The multi-view aware transformer and the introduction of confidence grids add additional value. The crucial novelty lies in how the whole system is designed to be scene-agnostic, highly efficient and decoupled from the 3DGS optimization - thus addressing the shortcomings of existing methods, like BilaRF, that involve computationally expensive joint optimization.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant practical significance. By decoupling the appearance harmonization from the core 3DGS optimization, it addresses a real bottleneck in terms of speed and scalability. The ability to pre-process images to ensure photometric consistency before feeding them into the 3DGS pipeline is a valuable contribution. Furthermore, the demonstration of cross-scene generalization, eliminating the need for retraining on new datasets, greatly boosts the practical applicability of this method. If their claims hold up in real-world applications, this has the potential to significantly improve the efficiency of 3D reconstruction pipelines.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Efficiency:** The main strength of the paper is the computational efficiency, which is crucial for integration into existing fast 3DGS pipelines.
    *   **Generalizability:** The ability to generalize across scenes is a major advantage over scene-specific optimization methods.
    *   **Clear Motivation:** The problem of photometric inconsistencies is well-motivated and its impact on 3D reconstruction is clearly explained.
    *   **Decoupled Design:** Decoupling the harmonization from the core 3DGS significantly streamlines the whole process.
    *   **Strong Results:** Quantitative and qualitative results generally demonstrate superior or comparable performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The results rely heavily on synthetic datasets. While they demonstrate real-world performance, it would be good to have more in depth real-world examples and comparisons, and address the domain gap.
    *   **Limited to Static Scenes:** As mentioned by the authors, they lack a mechanism to deal with dynamic objects. This is a clear limitation that could significantly impact performance in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Reference Image Sensitivity:** Performance relies somewhat on the selection of the reference image. How to best chose such an image should be better discussed.
    *   **Reliance on Existing Datasets:** The dependency on datasets generated using the DL3DV framework could be seen as a constraint, as the specific types of inconsistencies modeled may not fully capture the complexities encountered in truly uncontrolled environments.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more efficient and practical approach to multi-view appearance harmonization for 3D reconstruction. The transformer-based framework, combined with bilateral grids and confidence maps, can serve as a basis for future research in this area. More specifically the decoupled, fast nature will likely be adopted by fast 3DGS workflows.

**Justification:**

The paper contributes a practical solution to a well-defined problem. The core novelty is in the efficient and decoupled design which is likely to have a real-world impact. Limitations such as the dataset dependency, restricted dynamic scene handling, somewhat lessen the overall impact. Despite this, the paper addresses a critical need in the field, offers a solid solution, and demonstrates strong results, it also builds upon the existing work without being fundamentally revolutionary in its approach.
